IIE’ WRONG KIND FOR 
“"S.BUSINESS. 


, (By Edgar Wallace.) , 


|e 


1 have:a very high regard for 
Press agents, oasiy because: for 
nine months I was one myself. 
But the good Press agénta canbe 
numbered on the fingers of one 
hand. ‘They are peculiarly interest- 
ing and hard-worked people, who 
are expected.to perform miracles, 
That in part those miracles are 
performed at all fs due entirely to 
the extraordinarily generous at-|° 
titude which the Press adopts, to- 
wards thentrical ventures. : 
From . timé to time one rends 
bitter out-bursts on the part of dis- 
appointed managements * against 
the tyranny and unscrupulous, 
vindictiveness of: Fleet-street. To 
say that any bad play would’ have 
run, if it, had not been- for the 
notices, ia as ridiculous as to 5Ug- 
gest that a cab horse would have 
won the Derby if the Press.writ2rs 
had been unanimous in. pr 
hin, : ' 
" Managers have a childlike faith 
in the power of the printed. word. 
They believe that if their play can 
only. be mentioned in newspaper 
paragraphs, — business will © auto- 
matically ‘follow. They are ensily 
outraged and depressed by uncom- 
plimentary ‘references to the goods 
which they are ‘offering for sale; 
they are unduly elated by extrava- 
gant praise, even though they may 
know the praise is wholly, unde 
‘served, and will not be endorsed by 
‘the theatre-golng public after per- 
ronal inspection. 
. Spoiling the Mlusion. 
The truth is that certain kinda 
of “publicity ‘are very bad ,indeed 
for the theatre, and particularly for 
the play which has induced that 
publicity. The thentre-going pub- 
lic’ is curfously sensitive to the at- 
‘mosphere of a theatre. ° When it 
ig announced in the public Press, 
with or without sensational head- 
lines, that “Misa Blossie Highkick 
in leaving the’ cast of “Ihe Blue- 
nosed Baboon’ owing to a quarrel 
with the management as to whether 
she shall wear stockings or appear 
in her own natural and shapely 
Jegs unadorned hy. silk covering,” 
and that “Miss Dolly Twinkletoes 
is taking her place,” it is quite pos- 
sible that the news will thrill a 
section of the’ public, who take o 
morbid interest in the covering. of 
legs, and ‘that it will Insplre 
enthusiasm in the breasts of Mise 
Twinkletocs’. followers, They will 


should’ not 
thought the play was coming off; 
I was Inundated’ with offers for 
the theatre; every.management in 
London wanted me to relensa mem~ 
bers of my cnat—they applied for 
each one separately: about three 
times over! : 
A Two-edged Axe.. 
Another error that is made all 
too frequently is that the wide an- 
nouncement that the: Censor has | - 
banned a play gives the piece an 
especial value when the ban is 
lifted,. The other day a. gentlo- 
man announced his-intention of put. 
ting on a drama, in which there 
wus a sensational scence where 
a lady i8 stripped to the waist and 
nearly burnt with~a real red-hot 
iron. Whether the Censor objected 
to’the Indy's decollete’ or to the 
realness of the red-hot iron, is not 
explained. aoe ; 
The result of this wide advertise- 
ment ‘is that, when the play is 
eventually passed, and the lady, de- 
cently attired in an artificial silk}. 
nightie,: ia burnt by a property 
iron, the people who would have 
been thrilled if no fuss had been 
made about the burning will yawn 
and be disapointed. . 
Never tell an audience what 
they're missing. And never, never 
take the trouble to explain that an 
act of any, kind isn’t os: good’ as_it 
might have been, owing to that in- 
tarfering man, Cromer. It is|- 
stupid to dash into print ‘and tell 
the public “We were going to «show 
you something awfully good, bub 
unfortunately you'll have’ to 
satisfied with -a pale imitation.” 
All publicity, that concentrates 
upon a-atunt or some apedial fens. 
ture of a play Is bad, The stunts 
in “The Spider” killed it, as-I res-

MIRROR TRICK FOR 
‘CITY PIGEONS. 
‘ALICE-BLUNDERLAND! RUSE 
OF THE TRAPPERS, 


Kighty-two City pigeons, roused a 
‘from their sleep outside St. Paul's 


Cathedral and the Royal. Ex- 
change in, the early hours of the 
morning recenfly.— 


Looked into mirrors eiaced in 
their: midst, Mistook thein reflec. 
Stepped 
forward to none friends, and— 
Were. trapped. 


. This ruse, Vv Witnessed by a hand- 
ful of early. City workers, was the 
latest development in the official 
campaign, started Inst. December, 
to reduce the City’ ‘8 pigeon popula- 
tion. \

been captured.

has trapped about 1,500 ‘pigeons. 
Hic hopes to capture from 600 to 
1,000 more in the next few months. 


~ LETTER GOLF. 


Don't show. your, HAND but 
keep your best FOOT forward. 


1—The {dea of letter golf is to 
change one word to another and 
do it in par, a given number of 
strokes, Thus to change COW to 
HEN, in three’ strokes, cow, 
HOW, HEW, ‘HEN. 

2—You can «change only one 
letter _at a time. ‘ 

, 8—-You must have. a complete, 
word, of common’ usage, for each 
jump. . Slang words and abbrevia- 
tions don’t count. 

4—The order of letters cannot 
be changed...

another page of this issue.

necessary, ‘foolish and dangerous 
obstruction still persists, We are 
happy to record that ‘there have 
been ‘fewer accidents in this re-{° 
xion during the past few months, 
which shows that there are some 
Chinese pedestrians’ who are 
waking up to the fact that there 
are ‘such things as motor cara In 
this Colony. In addition to need- 
less loitering, the ‘Chinese are 
often, guilty of hurrying’ across 
the road without. troubling to see 
what trafic is approaching. It 
has been suggested that, a remedy 
might be found .by placing street 
refuges in Queen’s Road Enst and 
in yarious parts of the Colony, but 
considered opinion is that local 
conditiond are all’ against their 
employment, In the first place, 
they would very Inrgely be 
ignored, and with a’ transient 
population such as comes and goes 
in Hongkong it, would be very: dif- 
ficult, ‘indeed: to educate the 
Chinese up to the idea of cross- 
ing atrecta only at the refuges. 
Further, refuges are themselves 
an obstruction and in other places 
have. been found. dangerous to 
night drivers, We read in a 
Wome Journal. that in Berlin It fs 
proposed to’ abolish strect Island 
refuyes for pedestrians and: re- 
place them by ordinary: white 
lines, A number of such. lines 
are in existence in this Colony but 
they would scarcely be suitable 
for the problem of Queen's’ Road 
East. We suggest that one- or 
two extra police officers might be 
nlnced. on duty in this thorough- 
fare with instructions to 
encourage people to/keep out. of

syn- 
two 
coils, two condonsers and n high 
tension distributor cap with two 
segments for each cylinder, from 
which cables run to the two plugs, 
located ‘one wpposite the other in 
each combustion. chamber. The 
engine thus has two practically 
separate ignition systems, and will 
operate on either of them if the 
other fails; the battery, the awitch 
and the high tension part of the 
distribubor ‘being tho only units 
common to both systems, Beside 
‘“nerensed réllability, this twin- 
spark system has the advantages 
of improving acceleration and fuel 
‘economy, combustion being. mora 
nearly complete when the charge 
is ignited at two points than at 
one only. Careful tests indicate 
that, two-point ignition increnses 
the output of an engine or enables 
the same output to be obtained 
with less expenditure of f uel,

‘Indfanapolis, April 19.—Auto-

That the supercharger Is going 
to be adopted soon as dn Im- 


{portant part of the regular pro- 


duction automobile, there is little 
doubt. It has been tested and 
proven at ‘various races’ and {8 
almost ready for popular use. 

By means of this apparatus arr 
engine ean be speeded up: to’ al- 
most twich its rate today, withgut 
choking for want of proper and | 
sufficient - fue] mixture. The 
supercharger is designed to force 


enough fuel, and {n the . proper

high speeds, into the engine 80 a8 
to keep it running without a miss. 

If superchargers are installed in’ 
automobiles, saya 


much smaller engines can be used. 

“Why,” asks Schwitzer, “should 
80 per cent. of all passenger car 
operators be penalized by exces- 
sive car weight and fuel consump- 


H |tion when they drive 90 per cent. 


of the time at less than 36° miles 
an, hour? About 960 pounds of 
engine weight and six to ten 
inches of wheelbase are added to 
cars to secure the performance 
which is ‘utilized for perhaps 20 
per cent. of the total drivlay time.” 


. While testing a car. with a 
supercharger, recently, Schwitzer 
anid he was able to accelerate past 
th case, and driving’ 
between 36 and 60 miles an how

, Are women motorists a menace? 
Some are—but, then, so ard some 
men. “All who have studied this: 


| questfon with an open mind agreo 


that the large and ever-increasing 
number of women drivers are de- 
veloping a! very “suro and very 
pound-road sense. a 


-Many ‘of ‘them cannot’ say Why 


H they do the right thing at the right 


moment,’ but then, neither can a 
good ‘many ‘other efficient (workers. 
In everything woman: acts more 
by ‘instinct than by reason—a mos! 
valuable nsdot to any driver, male 
or female. . an Bt 


Indeed, woman's ‘mental make- 
up exhibits just .those qualities: 
which.the godd driver should pos- 
seas. When she takes to motoring 
her chief alm {s to learn to drive. 
She concentrates her whole atten- 
tion and her enormous enthusiasm 
on: mastering. those details which 
make the perfect. motorist. 


. She may not caro a bit for pears, 
pistons, magnctos. and the other 
grisly details about which mere 


man: concerns himself. She is out |’ 


to achiove results, and those results 
are almost invariably good. «. 


Woman has’ o natural aptitude 
for driving. .. Her touch is delicate; 
her sense of fitness .very strong. 
She does things properly. because 
it comes naturally to her to do’ so: 
She is not content just to “pick 
things’ up;” she studies © driving 
with keenness, The result?’ It 
enn be observed on all our roads 
to-day, provided one has the eyes 
and will to ‘seo. . 


There was a time when the sight 
of & woman at the wheel alarmed 
all who witnessed it. Men drivers 
gave her a wide berth, The fact 
that sho was a’ woman was accept- 
ed as sufficient excuse for most sins 
of omission and commission... That 
‘spirit lingers still, in spite of what 
women drivers did during the war. 


But medern motoring Eve does 
not caré for that .sort of thing. 
Rightly, she-is a driver firat and a 
woman second, She is on the road 
because she has a right to be there 
—because she ean drive well. She 
neither asks nor expects preferen- 
tial’ treatment,’ though o signal of 
distress is still sufficient to. bring 
masculine assistance to her side, 


What woman is not proud? It 
ia that pride—pride in her own 
ability—that ‘is ‘makin’ ‘her the 
perfect driver, When she js on the 
road sho fa far more concerned with 
her’ driving than the ‘effect she is 
creating .on the passing male. It 
is here that she exccls, One job 
at a time is enough for her, 


In the ballroom or. the restaurant 
she is In her kingdom. Then’ she 
expects the homage which is her 
due; but on the road~not a bit 
of it! She is just a unit in the 
army of road-users, courteous t6 
others and expecting the same in 
‘return, ¢ 


That may sound a sweep. 
ing statement, for: the old 
gibe that women know nothing 
about’ the team apirit dics 
hard. But’ it fs true! Watch 
a woman in a sports car! She 
doesn't uttempt spectacular stunts 
to the danger of other people. It's 
the young blood, in plus-fours and 
a college muffler who misuses the 
power of the super-car. 


The secret of good . driving’ is 
simply the ubility to get there in 
the shortest time and with no dan- 
ger; to others. If. s0me women 
haven’t learnt it, that’s because 
they haven't had, time. The same 
rule applies to men. ee 


There's no inherent reason why 
women.can’t become drivers every. 
bit as efficient as men, They are 
proving ‘it, not only on the road, 
but on the track, where resource, 
quick thinking and initiative aro 
constantly demanded.

"Notes. © t 

Worldng with the clutch, in the 
process of making the ‘engine 


move the automobile, fs the trans- 
mission. i | 


tes 


‘This consists of an arrange- 
ment of gears by. which a car mhy 
pick un speed in gradual steps, or 


.back up when desired... eer 


Knowledge of how these gears 
work may help toward ‘elimina- 
tion of the scraping nojscs wwe 
hear when poor, operation causes 
them:-to clash." In fact, it may 


4 {avoid the possibility of stripping 


‘some of the tecth from the gears 
and, so making the entire trans- 
mission worthless. ~ : : 

. a oo Sr . 

The gears are so arranged in 
the transmission that, when the 
Jever is in neutral position, they 
do not mesh. All that fs happen- 
ing, while: tho ‘engine is running 
and tho clutch is engaged, ia the 
revolution of the main’. driving 
gear on the engine shaft and the 
countershaft gears with - which 
contact {s mada. to those on the 
propeller or drive shaft. : 


Since the- operating gears are 
not meshed, the clutch mny be 
engaged. But.when the lever is to 
be thrown into first, to mesh the 
jarge sliding gear on the propel- 
ler shaft with the smaller one on 
the” running: countershaft, . the 
clutch must first ‘be disengaged 
completely—the foot pedal must 
be clear down to thd’ floor board 
—or there will. be a scraping of 
gears, ao straining and possibly 
stalling of the engine. That, too, 
is how gears are stripped of their 
tecth and rendéred useless. 
ue * Aa . 


This is what happens when the} 


lever As shifted: 


‘The engine has been running at 
a.normal speed, while the lever is 
in.neutral and the car is standing 
still.- While this is going on, how- 
ever,’, the engine js driving the 
gears on the countershaft at:the 
same speed it is going. 


When the clutch is disengaged, 
the engine is separated from the 
countershaft. gears and leaves 
these to revolve on the momentum 
it“ has given. them. . While they 
are revolving, at a: comparatively 
slow speed, the low gears can be 
meshed with the sliding gear on 
the propeller shaft, the - clutch 
may then be Jet in slowly and the 
car started. : : 


* Having picked up enough mo- 
mentum, the clutch is disengaged 
again—separating' the — engine 
from the transamiasion—the alid- 
ing gear is moved to the next 
faster » countershaft gear, the 
clutch {8 let in again, and off goca 
the, car at a higher speed. 


* 


' At thd highest speed, third, the 


engine is connected directly with. 


the propeller shaft, by the same 
clutch and lever action. ‘For this. 
reagon, the engine may be slowed 
up or speeded up in direct pro- 
portion with the road speed de- 
sired, : ” 


But if the car is slowed down 
too far, there will come a point 
in the contact where the pull .of 
the car will be too much for the 
engine and the motor ‘will stall: 
To prevent this, the transmission 
must be shifted back into neutral 
position, and then’ into the next 
lower. gear, fa 


In lower’ gear, the gear’ wheel 
directly connected to the engine is 
smaller than the one connected to 
the propeller shaft, thus accom- 
modating: the higher speed of the 
engine.to the lower speed of the

As the average traffic density on 
the roads increases, so does: the 
need for rapid acceleration ‘be- 
come ‘more ‘and,more pressing. 
With fourwheel brakes (and servo 
motors with -which to operate 
them) the stopping powers. of 4 
car are now. better than those of- 
nny other vehicle, so that, without 
inconveniencing other road users, 
the driver whose. car will ac- 
eelorate rapidly can offen pass 
others and get back again on the. 
noir sida of the road in safety 
where ,thé same manpenvre, at- 
tempted -on-a slower vehicle, would 


be fraught with daiger, or ‘even | 


disaster., And whilemnny and 
various are the views: held by 
motorists. concerning controversial 
topics; such as suspension, stecr- 
ing, and types of bodywork, it 
would be difficult to find any one 
who did not relish a quick -gate- 
away as a feature in the‘ per- 
formance of his ear, A lively and 
responsive’ machine which, at o 
touch of the throttle, surges for- 
ward with rapidly: Incrensing 
specd {gs a possession greatly to be 
prized. e 
Power-weight Relation. ; 

In -apeaking of rapid accelera- 
tion moet people naturally think of 
engine power as being the most 
important requirement, whereas 
only a little reflection should be 
needed to show that the weight 
of ‘the «vehicle has an equally 
potent influence upon its live- 
liness. Acceleration, in fact, is 
exactly proportional to what is 
known. ag the .power-weight ‘ratio, 
and thia ratio can obviously be bet- 
tered by increasing the power or 
by reducing the weight. In many 
small cara, of medium ‘size the 
engine may develop about 40 h.p., 
and the total weight will be about 
one ton, giving, in round figures, 
a ratio of. 2 bhp. per Hundred- 
weight. To get a better accclera- 


tion than is possible with such a! 


ear the designer has two methods 
available; to incrense the. output 
of the engine, or to reduce: the 
weight of the vehicle. Thus, if the 


weight can be cut down to 16cwt.,| | 


the acceleration would be exactly 
the same as though the weight 
had remained unchanged andthe 


hp. ane : 

~ . Effect of Overloading. '. . 

This point is emphayiséd. . be- 
cause in many enses the perform 
ance.pf a car has been quite spoilt 
by ‘overloading it with.” cumber- 
some ‘bodywork, and then, to 
bring the acceleration back to nor- 
ma}, the designer has been forced 
to wring a few more “horses” from 
an unwilling engine, .a practice 
which may easily maoke.a smooth- 
running power unit Into:a rough 
one. Nowadays, ‘however, with 
light. six-cyclinder chassia and 
fabric bodywork, power-weight 
‘ratios, show signs of an all round 
improvement. he esd 

It is also worth mentioning that 
when an éngine fs causing ‘a-car 
to accelerate it must ‘also provide 
power to increase the speed of the 
main rotating parts, such as the 
flywheel, ‘tranamission shafting 


and road’ wheels, ‘The rotational |- 


inertia of these parts {a not very 
great compared with the inortia 
of the ear as a whole, but never- 


.theless makes i difference to the. 


performance obtainable, 


car.: At higher specd, the engine | 


is able to exert a proportionately 
greater pull on the car’ through 
the low gears and thus 
start the car again or keep it pull- 
ing’ up.a steep hill-or in slow 
traffic. ; : a

Washing Fabric Bodies. 


Many motorists complain . that 
the fabric body {s not so easy to 
clean as an ordinary coachbuilt job, 
but it is pointed out by a4 well- 
known concern that the, reverse 
should be the case if the right 
methods are adopted. 


If & hose is available and the car 
is very muddy, it should be “sluiced 
down” at the outset, allowing 
a little time-for the water to soften 
the adhering mud. All that is 
then required is a bucket of warm 
water with plenty of soap In it and 
a soft brush: The meticulous care 
to see that there is no grit on the 
ear, which is so necessary when 
dealing with a varnished surface, 


is not called for on a fabric body. |} 
The ‘surface of the fabric is}] 
celluloze, and ihe immunity from 
damuge by scratching © that 
ia claimed for. the cellulose- 
finished car is also an_ attri- 
bute ‘of the fabric, If - the 
dirt .is particularly stubborn, 
as for instance when the car has 
been left for two or three days 
fore washing, a° teaspoonful ,of 
paraffin added to the water will 
render the work much easier and no 
damage will be done to the fabric. 
After washing and ledthering, 
che surface should be polished with! 
a dry duster, when its natural lus- 
tre will be restored, .The use of 
polishes—says the concern in ques- 
tion—is unnecessary and, in fact, 
is not recommended, as if wax is 
allowed to lodge in the pores of the 
material it is apt to hold dirt which 
necessitates very’ hard ‘rubbing to 
remove it, , ' 
Washing a fabric body’ in’ this 
way rendera the work easy, and it 
certainly has the advantage of not. 
calling ‘for a prolonged use of ‘cold 
water, always an unpleasant mat-}: 
ter, especially in wintry weather. 
Generally speaking, a Weymann 
body will ve found easier to clean 
chan a‘body , on which the fabrie 
had been fixed to the sheet-eteel 
foundations. This arises from the 
fact that the fabric not being back-

A ‘friend of Charles Dickens 
and a gchoolboy of. the time of the 
great Exhibition of 1861, Mr. C. 
Tijou writes for The Daily 
Chrontele on life, as it has ap- 
peared to him over the space of 
more than 80 years. ees 


. Mr. Tijou, as a lad, told Dickens 
ef his adventures in the hands of 


bably' provided the “author with 
materia} for his conception of 
Squeera, ‘ : 
“Dickens,” he writes, “was per- 
AGC ....0e Although only a child, 
I wag deeply impressed by his as~ 
tuteness and insight.” ” 


‘Mind Changing, 


Why is it, I wonder, that women 
are so fond of changing . their. 
minds? . 

When asked. this question a 
short time ago, a young lady of 
my ‘acquaintance remarked — that 
she thought men imagined this to 
be the case because they, on their 
part, had none to change. 

She supposed that in the days 
when the world was young, some. 
renegade from the female camp 
made the suggestion’ in spiteful 
mood, and’ that it had just stuck, 
men being unable Lo get the idea 
‘out of their thick heads ever since, 
despite all evidence to the con- 


Be this as it may, in reviewing 
the events of the last half century 
it is the profound apparent differ- 
ence between the manners and 
customs-of modérn women’ and 
those of days gone by that most 
impresses, folk who have lived to 
see.them pass. , 7: 

“Ringlet Fronts” would be 
Jaughed to -scorn by the modern 


| damsel, who disdains her hair to 


the extent of shaving Jike-a poodle, 


{And yet Victorian maidens would |. 


have hidden discreet amiles be- 
hind ‘Ince handkerchiefs could 


| they have seen the sprightly valves 
‘}of modern youth, 


Which seoms 
to suggest that after all my young 
friend may. have had some righ 

upon her side, a 


Acute Observer. 


One great man of the nineteenth 
century, whose friendship {t was 
my privilege to enjoy, would have 


acen little’ difference ‘between his | 


own contemporaries and the men 
and women of to-day. 

Charles Dickens was, perhaps, 
the acutest observer of the age. 
He probed far beneath the un- 
{mportant externals by which 80 
many people judge their neigh- 


bours, and, although only a child, 


I was deeply impressed with his 
astuteness and insight. 

Dickens was a slight man, long- 
haired with pointed whiskers and 
with eyes that seemed for ever 
searching.. Many’ an evening be- 
fore | was packed off: to bed, as 
was the custom in those days when 
the family had guests, He drew 
me to his side, chatting to me and 
asking me’ questions: about. my, 
life at school. . 4 

For many years J fully believed 
that it was. these talks which. had 
given the great. novelist his [dea 
for the character of “Squeers” in 
“Nicholas Nickleby.” : 


t 


At my college we had a master 
whose habit it was to prolong 
thrashings for half an hour - or 
more, He seemed to take a de- 
light in ‘spinning out the agony, 
Pausing to take snuff, he would 
carry on with his lecture, and.then 
turn to the thrashing again when 
his arm was rested. ‘ 

- Questioning me closely upon this 
‘pedagogue's latest exploits, Dic- 
kens was especially amused aot the 
plans I devised to be revenged on 


the end of class the doctor’ was 


with instrudtions to buy a fresh 


|| stock of snuff and refill his box. 


This gave me my opportunity. 
Stenling back into the classroom 
when he had disappeared, down 
the passage, I used to sweep up 
the grains -with which. his...desk 
wag liberally ‘strewn,’ refill. : the 
box,'and pocket: the’ money. . 
pT hese | -lttlo, “windfalls, were 
specially welcome, ’::for: in those 
days: schoolboy ha Bs 
the ‘luxury :of>'

The Kuala Lumpur Outrage. 
. The following extract from the 
Evangelie de la Popular is of. in- 


terest, as showing that the bomb-| 


ing of the Chinese Protectorate at 
Kuala Lumpur was originally in- 
tended to be an attempt on the 
Nfe of the High Cammissioner 
himself: ~ ret as ei 

“After holding a general mect- 
Ing in the Malay Peninsula several 
men were arrested by the British 
Government, All the anarchists 
were bitterly grieved and devised 
a scheme for revenge. It was to 
bomb the Governor of Singapore. 
They decided, to carry: out the 
scheme ot January 3, 1925, when 


the Governor of Singapore,. went |: 


on a tour of the 3s. find PMS. 
Comrade‘ Miss Wong So-ying un- 
dertook to throw thé: bomb: - and 
comrade Yat Mun to shoot with 
the pistol The two comrades 
tried to meet the Goyernor but in 
vain, “They then changed their 
plan and went to Kuala Lumpur 
io bomb the Secretary: for Chinese 
Affairs, M.S." , ‘

NEW TURN: TO BATTLE 
BEFORE MINE BALLOT. 


- Nearly 30 Labour-,M.P.'s took 
part yesterday in meetings in Not- 
tingham and district ax u prelude 
to the Trades Union Congress ballot 
of miners. ; : 

The ballot is to decide whether 
the men ish to belong to tie Notts 
Miners’ Association or the Notts 
Miners’ Industrial Non-Political 
Union, started after the general 
strike by Mr. George Spencer, 
MSP. : a 
A bitter platform battle is being 
waged, and it was stated 
that Mx. Spencer had issued a writ 
for slander against Mr, A.J. Cook, 
the miners’ leader, : 

Mr. Cook. said “that the 
isste was, Shall the employers des- 
troy the freedom of association of 
tae employees? . They were male 
ing more:Cooks. If the employers 
had not victimised him, he would 
not have been federation secretary. 

In a couple of months, Mr. Cook 
declared, the owners would throw 
Mr. Spencer on the scrap heap, 

Mr. Havelock Wilson told the In- 
dustrial Union that Mr. Cook had 
wreeked one of the greatest 
organisations the world had ever 


seen, “ 
He challenged Cook and Smith to 
say how much money they had given 
to rebels of the Scumen's Union to 
carry. on litigation. - '

-” NOBLE ART OF 
BARROW WORK. 


eae! 
' OF. PUSHING. 


People who push barrows about 


that Government experts have 
worked out the cost—in oxygen, 
of course, not Nquid refreshment. 


“The Physiological Cost of the | tet. 


Muscular Movements involved in 


by the Stationery Office (18.), and 
gives the detailed experiments of 


worker, which he carried gut for 
the Indystrial Fatigue Research 
Board. in 

To standardise the experiment, 

barrow londs of bricks: were used 
on the sort of barrows used at 
brickworks. ; 
A trained barrow worker, ob- 
tained from tlic Employment Ex- 
change, was the anbject of the ex- 
periments, and after a light break- 
fast he was fitted up with a 
scientific apparatus for measuring 
oxygen consumption ealled a 
“Douglas bag.” - 

He was,told to'wheel the loaded 
barrow in spurts of ten metres 
distances of 50 and 100 metres, 
ang the excess of oxygen used up 
was noted, Then he was made to 
cover 150 metres without stops at 
a normal brisk walk. . 

It was proved that barrow- 
wheeling is a skilled job, Only 
the fully trained men can adequ- 
ately contro! and balance the 
barrow, with the minimum tension 
on his. arms, while at the same 
time maintaining his body in the 
position ~ which . permits. -of 
maximum comfort and efficiency 
for the muscular movements in- 
volved. 

As the result of the experiments 
it was found that an uninterrupted 
ron is most important. One stop 
muy involve 30 per cent. increase 
in. oxygen cost of the work. Cer- 
inin improvements in barrow ,de- 
sign have also been suggested.
